\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

The denominator is already factored as \( x - 1 \).

To find the limit 

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1},
\]

we can simplify the expression by factoring both the numerator and the denominator.

**Step 1: Factor the Numerator**

The numerator \( x^3 - 1 \) is a difference of cubes, which can be factored using the formula \( a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2) \). Here, \( a = x \) and \( b = 1 \), so:

\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

**Step 2: Simplify the Expression**

Now, substitute the factored form of the numerator back into the original expression:

\[
\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1} = \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
\]

We can cancel out the \( (x - 1) \) terms in the numerator and denominator, provided \( x \neq 1 \):

\[
\frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1
\]

**Step 3: Compute the Limit**

Now that we've simplified the expression, we can directly substitute \( x = 1 \):

\lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = (1)^2 + 1 + 1 = 3

1. **Behavior of the Numerator**:  
   As \( x \to \infty \), \( e^x \) grows exponentially.

2. **Behavior of the Denominator**:  
   As \( x \to \infty \), \( x^2 \) grows polynomially.

3. **Comparison of Growth Rates**:  
   Exponential functions grow much faster than polynomial functions as \( x \) becomes very large.

4. **Conclusion**:  
   Since \( e^x \) dominates over \( x^2 \) for large values of \( x \), the ratio \( \frac{e^x}{x^2} \) increases without bound as \( x \to \infty \).

The limit can be found by expressing tan(x) in terms of sin(x) and cos(x), then using known limits.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express tan(x) in terms of sine and cosine:**
   \[
   \frac{\tan x}{x} = \frac{\sin x}{x \cdot \cos x}
   \]

2. **Split the expression into two separate limits:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{\sin x}{x} \cdot \frac{1}{\cos x} \right) = \left( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} \right) \cdot \left( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{\cos x} \right)
   \]

3. **Evaluate each limit:**
   - The standard limit \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1\).
   - As \(x\) approaches 0, \(\cos x\) approaches 1, so \(\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{\cos x} = 1\).

4. **Multiply the results:**
   \[
   1 \cdot 1 = 1
   \]

**Answer:**  
The limit is 1.

- tan(0) = 0

**Solution:**

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll apply basic differentiation rules.

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
   - The derivative of \( -5x \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \) is:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\text{6x - 5}}

To find the derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \), we can use the power rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express the Function Using Exponents:**
   Rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) as \( x^{-1} \).

2. **Apply the Power Rule:**
   The power rule states that \( \frac{d}{dx} [x^n] = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).
   
3. **Differentiate:**
   Here, \( n = -1 \). Applying the power rule:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} [x^{-1}] = (-1) \cdot x^{-1 - 1} = -x^{-2}
   \]
   
4. **Simplify the Result:**
   Rewrite \( -x^{-2} \) as \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

To find the second derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \), we will perform differentiation twice.

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative
Using the power rule for differentiation, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = n x^{n-1} \), we differentiate each term of the function:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) - 3 \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) + \frac{d}{dx}(x)
\]

Calculating each derivative separately:
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) = 4x^3 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^2 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \)

Putting it all together:

\[
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative
Now, we differentiate the first derivative to find the second derivative \( f''(x) \):

\[
f''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) - \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) + \frac{d}{dx}(1)
\]

Calculating each term:
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) = 12x^2 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) = 18x \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0 \)

Therefore, the second derivative is:

\[
f''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x
\]

### Final Answer
The second derivative of \( f(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \) is:

\[
\boxed{12x^2 - 18x}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we use the product rule. The product rule states that if \( f(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then \( f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x) \).

Here, let:
- \( u(x) = \cos x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- The derivative of \( \cos x \) is \( -\sin x \), so \( u'(x) = -\sin x \).
- The derivative of \( \ln x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \), so \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

Now, apply the product rule:
\[ f'(x) = (-\sin x) \cdot (\ln x) + (\cos x) \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right) \]

Simplifying, we get:
\[ f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x} \]

The final derivative is \( f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x} \).

Let \( y = x^x \).

Taking the natural logarithm: \( \ln(y) = x \ln(x) \).

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1) \]

\[ \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \]

The function we are given is \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \). To find its derivative, we can use the chain rule or the quotient rule.

**Using the Chain Rule:**
1. Rewrite the function as \( f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \).
2. Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \), so \( f(u) = u^{-1} \).
3. The derivative of \( f(u) \) with respect to \( u \) is \( -u^{-2} \).
4. The derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x \).
5. Multiply the derivatives: \( f'(x) = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \).

**Using the Quotient Rule:**
1. Let numerator be 1 and denominator be \( x^2 + 1 \).
2. Apply the quotient rule formula:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{(0)(x^2 + 1) - (1)(2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2} = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

Both methods yield the same result.

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we can use the chain rule. 

**Solution:**

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Outer function: \( e^{u} \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate using the chain rule:**
   
   The derivative of \( e^{u} \) with respect to \( u \) is \( e^{u} \).
   
   Multiply this by the derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \):
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x^2}) = e^{x^2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^2)
   \]

3. **Compute the derivative of the inner function:**
   
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x
   \]

   \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x^2}) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}
   \]

Let me compute each part step by step to avoid mistakes.

To find the second derivative of \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll follow these steps:

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative**

We start by differentiating \( f(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x^2} \right)
\]

Using the chain rule, where the outer function is \( e^u \) and the inner function is \( u = x^2 \):

\[
f'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative**

Next, we differentiate \( f'(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
f''(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( 2x e^{x^2} \right)
\]

We'll use the product rule here, which states that:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}(uv) = u'v + uv'
\]

Let \( u = 2x \) and \( v = e^{x^2} \). Then:

- \( u' = 2 \)
- \( v' = \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x^2} \right) = 2x e^{x^2} \)

Applying the product rule:

\[
f''(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot 2x e^{x^2}
\]

Simplify the expression:

\[
f''(x) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2} = (2 + 4x^2) e^{x^2}
\]

**Final Answer**

\[
\boxed{ \, f''(x) = (2 + 4x^2)e^{x^2} \, }
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's apply this to our function step by step.

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = x \\
   v(x) = 1 + x^2
   \]

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = 1 \\
   v'(x) = 2x
   \]

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the numerator:**
   \[
   1(1 + x^2) - x(2x) = 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
   \]

5. **Write the final derivative:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

To find the derivative of \( \sec x \):

1. Express \( \sec x \) as \( \frac{1}{\cos x} \).
2. Apply the quotient rule: if \( f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)} \), then \( f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} \).
3. Here, \( g(x) = 1 \) and \( h(x) = \cos x \). Thus, \( g'(x) = 0 \) and \( h'(x) = -\sin x \).
4. Plugging into the quotient rule: 
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{0 \cdot \cos x - 1 \cdot (-\sin x)}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
   \]
5. Simplify using trigonometric identities:
   \[
   \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x} = \sec x \tan x
   \]

The derivative of \( \sec x \) is:

Here's a step-by-step solution:

\[
\begin{align*}

&= 18 - 15 + 1 \\

Okay, so I have this problem here where I need to find the limit of sin(x)/x as x approaches zero. Hmm, I remember from my calculus class that there's a standard limit for this expression, which is 1. But wait, let me think through it step by step to make sure I understand why that's the case.

First off, when x is approaching zero, both the numerator sin(x) and the denominator x are getting very small. It might be tempting to say that since both are approaching zero, their ratio approaches 1 because they're of similar magnitude. But actually, it's not just about them being small; it's about how fast each function approaches zero.

I recall that for small angles (in radians), sin(x) is approximately equal to x. So as x gets closer to zero, sin(x) ≈ x. That means sin(x)/x should be roughly x/x = 1. Therefore, intuitively, the limit makes sense to be 1.

By comparing areas or using triangles, we can establish inequalities that help us bound sin(x)/x between two functions whose limits are both 1 as x approaches zero.

Specifically, from geometry, I remember that for small positive angles, cos(x) < sin(x)/x < 1. As x approaches zero, cos(x) approaches 1 because cos(0) is 1. So if the middle term is bounded between two quantities that both approach 1, by the squeeze theorem, it must also approach 1.

That's a solid argument supporting the standard limit result. Alternatively, using Taylor series expansion for sin(x), we have:

Dividing both sides by x gives:

As x approaches zero, the higher power terms (like x^2, x^4) become negligible, so sin(x)/x approaches 1. This is another way to confirm the limit.

lim_{x→0} sin(x)/x = lim_{x→0} [d/dx sin(x) / d/dx x] = lim_{x→0} (cos(x)/1) = cos(0) = 1.

Wow, that's a straightforward application of L’Hospital’s Rule. So multiple methods converge to the same result: the limit is indeed 1.

The limit of the expression √(x² + 2x) - x as x approaches infinity is calculated using rationalization and series expansion, both leading to a result of 1.

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Rationalize the Expression:**
   Start by multiplying the numerator and denominator by the conjugate of the expression:
   
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x = \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x)(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
   \]
   
   The numerator simplifies to:
   
   \[
   (x^2 + 2x) - x^2 = 2x
   \]
   
   So, the expression becomes:
   
   \[
   \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
   \]

2. **Simplify the Denominator:**
   Factor out \( x \) from the square root in the denominator:
   
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = x\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
   \]
   
   Therefore, the expression becomes:
   
   \[
   \frac{2x}{x\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + x} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( \frac{2}{x} \) approaches 0:
   
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} = \sqrt{1 + 0} = 1
   \]
   
   Thus, the expression simplifies to:
   
   \[
   \frac{2}{1 + 1} = 1
   \]

**Answer:**  
The limit is \( \boxed{1} \).

To find the limit of (x² - 4)/(x - 2) as x approaches 2, I notice that substituting x = 2 directly results in a 0/0 indeterminate form. This suggests that factoring might be necessary to simplify the expression.

The (x - 2) terms in the numerator and denominator cancel each other out, leaving me with x + 2.

To find the limit of the expression \(\frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2}\) as \(x\) approaches 2, follow these steps:

1. **Factor the Numerator:**
   
   The numerator \(x^2 - 4\) is a difference of squares and can be factored as:
   \[
   x^2 - 4 = (x - 2)(x + 2)
   \]

2. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Substitute the factored form into the original expression:
   \[
   \frac{x^2 - 4}{x - 2} = \frac{(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x - 2}
   \]
   
   Notice that \((x - 2)\) is a common factor in both the numerator and the denominator. Cancel out this common term:
   \[
   \frac{\cancel{(x - 2)}(x + 2)}{\cancel{x - 2}} = x + 2
   \]
   
3. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   Now, substitute \(x = 2\) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 2}} (x + 2) = 2 + 2 = 4
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{4}

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Limit Form:**
   The expression is lim_{x→0+} (ln(1+x))/x, which results in 0/0 when substituting x = 0. This is an indeterminate form suitable for L'Hôpital's Rule.

2. **Apply L'Hôpital's Rule:**
   Differentiate the numerator and denominator with respect to x.
   - d/dx [ln(1+x)] = 1/(1+x)
   - d/dx [x] = 1

3. **Evaluate the New Limit:**
   The limit becomes lim_{x→0+} [1/(1+x)] / 1 = lim_{x→0+} 1/(1+x) = 1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Problem:**
   Find the limit:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3}
   \]

2. **Use Taylor Series Expansion for tan(x):**
   The Maclaurin series expansion of tan(x) around 0 is:
   \[
   \tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \ldots
   \]
   
3. **Subtract tan(x) from x:**
   Subtract the series expansion of tan(x) from x:
   \[
   x - \tan x = x - \left( x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \ldots \right) = -\frac{x^3}{3} - \ldots
   \]
   
4. **Form the Limit Expression:**
   Substitute back into the limit:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{-\frac{x^3}{3}}{x^3} = \lim_{x \to 0} -\frac{1}{3} = -\frac{1}{3}
   \]

5. **Conclusion:**
   The limit evaluates to -1/3.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Express \( \frac{1}{x} \) using a negative exponent:**
   \[
   \frac{1}{x} = x^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Power Rule:**
   The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

3. **Differentiate \( x^{-1} \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left( x^{-1} \right) = -1 \cdot x^{-2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   -x^{-2} = -\frac{1}{x^2}
   \]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we apply the product rule:

1. Let \( u = \cos x \) and \( v = \ln x \).
2. Compute derivatives: 
   - \( u' = -\sin x \)
   - \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \)
3. Apply product rule:
   - \( f'(x) = u'v + uv' = (-\sin x)(\ln x) + (\cos x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \)

Thus, the derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

To find the derivative of \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^x) \), we can use logarithmic differentiation.

1. Let \( y = x^x \).
2. Take the natural logarithm of both sides: \( \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) \).
3. Simplify using logarithm properties: \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).
4. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \) (using the chain rule).
   - Right side: Apply the product rule to \( x \cdot \ln(x) \), resulting in \( 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
5. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \): \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).
6. Substitute \( y = x^x \): \( \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

The derivative is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{d}{dx}(x^x) = x^x (1 + \ln(x))}
\]

The second derivative of \( f(x) = x^2 e^x \) is found by applying the product rule twice. 

First derivative:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) \cdot e^x + x^2 \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

Second derivative:
\[
f''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(2x e^x) + \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 e^x)
\]
For each term, apply the product rule again:
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(2x e^x) = 2e^x + 2x e^x \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 e^x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x \)

Combine the results:
\[
f''(x) = (2e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x^2 e^x) = 2e^x + 4x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]
Factor out \( e^x \):
\[
f''(x) = (x^2 + 4x + 2)e^x
\]

**Final Answer**
The second derivative of \( f(x) = x^2 e^x \) is \(\boxed{(x^{2} + 4x + 2) e^{x}}\).

**Solution:**
We can use the chain rule for differentiation.

Let:
- \( u = x^2 + 1 \)
- \( f(u) = \frac{1}{u} = u^{-1} \)

First, differentiate \( f(u) \) with respect to \( u \):
\[
f'(u) = -u^{-2}
\]

Next, find the derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \):
\[
\frac{du}{dx} = 2x
\]

Now, apply the chain rule:
\[
\frac{df}{dx} = f'(u) \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( f(x) \) is:

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Outer function (\( f(u) \)): \( e^u \)
   - Inner function (\( u(x) \)): \( x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to \( u \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} e^u = e^u
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x
   \]

4. **Apply the chain rule by multiplying the derivatives:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = \frac{d}{du} e^u \cdot \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
   \]

5. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = 2x e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{2x e^{x^2}}

- The derivative of \( u = \ln x \) is \( u' = \frac{1}{x} \).
- The derivative of \( v = x^2 \) is \( v' = 2x \).

Now, I'll apply the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{\left( \frac{1}{x} \right) (x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
\]

\[
\left( \frac{1}{x} \right) (x^2) = x
\]
\[
(\ln x)(2x) = 2x \ln x
\]

The denominator is \( (x^2)^2 = x^4 \).

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), then its derivative \( f'(x) \) is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Let:
\[
u(x) = \ln x \\
v(x) = x^2
\]

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

Substitute \( u(x) \), \( v(x) \), \( u'(x) \), and \( v'(x) \) into the quotient rule formula:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{\left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression**

Simplify the numerator and denominator:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3}
\]

So, the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \) is:

\[
f'(x) = \boxed{\dfrac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1}x \), we start by setting \( y = \tan^{-1}x \). This implies \( \tan(y) = x \).

\[
\frac{d}{dx}[\tan(y)] = \frac{d}{dx}[x]
\]
Using the chain rule on the left side:
\[
\sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]
Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
\]
Since \( \tan(y) = x \), we use the identity \( \sec^2(y) = 1 + \tan^2(y) \):
\[
\sec^2(y) = 1 + x^2
\]
Thus:

\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's identify the parts of our function:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)

Now, find the derivatives of each part:
1. The derivative of \( u(x) = x \) is \( u'(x) = 1 \).
2. The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) is \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \).

Apply the product rule:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Substitute the derivatives into the formula:

\[
f'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]

Simplify each term:
- \( 1 \cdot \ln x = \ln x \)
- \( x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 1 \)

Combine the terms:

\[
f'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

The derivative of \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\) is found using the quotient rule:

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify u(x) and v(x):**
   - \(u(x) = \sin x\)
   - \(v(x) = x^2 + 1\)

2. **Compute the derivatives of u and v:**
   - \(u'(x) = \cos x\)
   - \(v'(x) = 2x\)

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   \[

To find the **second derivative** of \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative
We'll use the **chain rule** to differentiate \( f(x) \).

- Let \( g(x) = x^2 \).
- Then, \( f(x) = e^{g(x)} \).
  
The derivative of \( e^{g(x)} \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
f'(x) = e^{g(x)} \cdot g'(x)
\]

Compute \( g'(x) \):
\[
g'(x) = 2x
\]

So, the first derivative is:
\[
f'(x) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative
Now, we'll differentiate \( f'(x) \) to find \( f''(x) \).

We have:
\[
f'(x) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
\]

Use the **product rule** to differentiate \( f'(x) \):
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u(x) \cdot v(x)] = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
Where:
- \( u(x) = 2x \)
- \( v(x) = e^{x^2} \)

Compute the derivatives:
\[
u'(x) = 2
\]
\[
v'(x) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
f''(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f''(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot (2x \cdot e^{x^2})
\]

Simplify the expression:
\[
f''(x) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
\]
\[
f''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)
\]

### Final Answer
The second derivative of \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \) is:
\[
\boxed{2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)}

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \), we can use the method of conjugate multiplication to simplify the expression.

First, we multiply and divide by the conjugate:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right) = \lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \frac{\left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right) \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x \right)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x} \right)
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \frac{(x^2 + 2x) - x^2}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x} \right) = \lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x} \right)
\]

Next, we factor out \( x \) from the denominator:

\[
= \lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \frac{2x}{x \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1 \right)} \right)
\]

Simplifying further, we get:

\[
= \lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1} \right)
\]

As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( \frac{2}{x} \) approaches 0, so:

\[
= \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 0} + 1} = \frac{2}{1 + 1} = 1
\]

Thus, the limit is:

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

To find the limit of \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side, we analyze the behavior of the function as \( x \) gets closer to 0.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Understanding the Expression:**
   - The expression is \( \frac{1}{x^2} \).
   - As \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side (\( x \to 0^+ \)), \( x \) becomes a very small positive number.

2. **Analyzing the Denominator:**
   - When \( x \) is close to 0, \( x^2 \) is an even smaller positive number (since squaring a small number makes it even smaller).
   - For example:
     - If \( x = 0.1 \), then \( x^2 = 0.01 \).
     - If \( x = 0.01 \), then \( x^2 = 0.0001 \).

3. **Evaluating the Expression:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, \( x^2 \) approaches 0 as well.
   - Therefore, \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) becomes very large because you're dividing 1 by a very small number.

4. **Determining the Limit:**
   - Since \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) grows without bound as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side, the limit is infinity.
   - Mathematically, this is expressed as:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{1}{x^2} = +\infty
     \]

### Final Answer:

The limit does not exist in a finite sense but instead approaches positive infinity.

Let me express x * ln(x) as (ln(x)) / (1/x). Now, when x approaches 0+, ln(x) goes to -∞ and 1/x goes to +∞. Either way, this becomes an indeterminate form suitable for L'Hospital's Rule.

Applying L'Hospital's Rule requires differentiating the numerator and the denominator with respect to x.

The derivative of ln(x) is 1/x, and the derivative of 1/x is -1/x². So applying the rule, we get lim_{x→0+} (1/x) / (-1/x²). Simplifying this, it's equal to lim_{x→0+} (1/x) * (-x²/1), which simplifies further to lim_{x→0+} -x.

As x approaches 0 from the positive side, -x approaches 0. So lim_{x→0+} ln(y) = 0.

Now that we have the limit of ln(y) as 0, we can find y by exponentiating both sides. So y = e^{lim_{x→0+} ln(y)} which is e^0 = 1.

</think>

The limit of \( x^x \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side is found by evaluating:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} x^x
\]

We can rewrite this expression using natural logarithms to simplify the computation. Let’s set \( y = x^x \), then take the natural log of both sides:

\[
\ln(y) = \ln(x^x) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

Next, we find the limit as \( x \to 0^+ \):

\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} \ln(y) = \lim_{x \to 0^+} x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

This results in an indeterminate form of \( 0 \cdot (-\infty) \). By rewriting it as \( \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} \), we apply L'Hospital's Rule:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\ln(x)}{1/x} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{d}{dx}\ln(x)}{\frac{d}{dx}(1/x)} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{1/x}{-1/x^2} = \lim_{x \to 0^+} -x = 0
\]

Thus, \( \ln(y) \) approaches 0. Exponentiating both sides gives:

\[
y = e^{0} = 1
\]

Therefore, the limit is:

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

The limit can be evaluated by recognizing it as the definition of the derivative of ln(1 + x) at x = 0. Alternatively, applying L'Hospital's Rule also yields the same result.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Form:** The expression lim_{x→0+} (ln(1+x))/x resembles the definition of a derivative.
2. **Apply Derivative Concept:** Recognize that this limit is equivalent to f'(0) where f(t) = ln(1 + t).
3. **Compute the Derivative:** f'(t) = 1/(1+t). Therefore, f'(0) = 1/(1+0) = 1.

   - Both numerator and denominator approach 0 as x→0+, so apply L'Hospital's Rule.
   - Differentiate numerator: d/dx [ln(1+x)] = 1/(1+x).
   - Differentiate denominator: d/dx [x] = 1.
   - New limit after applying the rule: lim_{x→0+} (1/(1+x)) / 1 = 1.

\]

So, in both the numerator and the denominator, the highest degree term is \( x^2 \). Let's factor those out:

\[
\frac{x^2(1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}{x^2(1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}

\[

\]

To solve the limit problem step by step:

**Problem:**  
Find the limit for the following expression:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}
\]

**Solution:**

We are asked to evaluate the limit of a rational function as \( x \) approaches infinity. Here's how we can approach it:

1. **Identify the Degrees of the Polynomials:**  
   Both the numerator and the denominator are polynomials of degree 2.

2. **Focus on the Leading Terms:**  
   When evaluating limits at infinity for polynomials, the leading terms (the terms with the highest power of \( x \)) dominate the behavior of the function.

3. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by the Highest Power of \( x \):**  
   In this case, divide each term in both the numerator and denominator by \( x^2 \):
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Limit:**  
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, both \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach 0:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The limit simplifies to **1**, so the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{1}

The limit of the function \( \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - 1 \) divided by \( x - 1 \) as \( x \) approaches 1 does not exist. As \( x \) approaches 1 from above, the expression tends to positive infinity, and as \( x \) approaches 1 from below, it tends to negative infinity.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Function Near \( x = 1 \):**
   - Consider \( f(x) = \sqrt[3]{1 + x^3} - 1 \).
   - As \( x \) approaches 1, evaluate the behavior of both numerator and denominator.

2. **Evaluate the Numerator:**
   - When \( x = 1 \), \( f(1) = \sqrt[3]{1 + 1^3} - 1 = \sqrt[3]{2} - 1 \approx 0.26 \).
   - As \( x \) approaches 1, the numerator remains positive and constant.

3. **Analyze Denominator:**
   - The denominator is \( x - 1 \).
   - When approaching from above (\( x \to 1^+ \)), \( x - 1 \) becomes a small positive number.
   - When approaching from below (\( x \to 1^- \)), \( x - 1 \) becomes a small negative number.

4. **Determine the Behavior Near Each Side:**
   - From above (\( x > 1 \)): The numerator (~0.26) is positive, and the denominator (small positive) leads to \( +\infty \).
   - From below (\( x < 1 \)): The numerator remains approximately ~0.26, but the denominator (small negative) results in a large negative value (\(-\infty \)).

5. **Conclusion:**
   - Since left and right limits are not equal (one approaches \( +\infty \), the other \( -\infty \)), the two-sided limit does not exist.

**Final Answer:** The limit does not exist because as \( x \) approaches 1 from above, it tends to positive infinity, while approaching from below leads to negative infinity.

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll apply basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \) with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \) with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{6x - 5}

To find the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \), I recall that one of the fundamental rules in calculus is that the derivative of \( e^x \) is unique because it remains unchanged upon differentiation.

Using the definition of derivatives, we apply the limit process:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^{x+h} - e^x}{h}
\]

Factoring out \( e^x \), we get:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h}
\]

Since the limit of \( \frac{e^h - 1}{h} \) as \( h \) approaches 0 is 1, we have:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x
\]
</think>

To find the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \), we can use one of the fundamental rules in calculus.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Understand the Function:**
   We are given the function \( f(x) = e^x \).

2. **Apply the Derivative Rule for Exponential Functions:**
   The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is a well-known result in calculus.

3. **Compute the Derivative:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{e^{x}}

That's more formal and doesn't rely on the approximation for small h. So it looks like the derivative of ln x is indeed 1/x.

   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} = \frac{\cos^2 x + \sin^2 x}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x} = \sec^2 x.
   \]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \cos(x) \cdot \ln(x) \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Components:**
   - Let \( u(x) = \cos(x) \)
   - Let \( v(x) = \ln(x) \)

2. **Find the Derivatives of Each Component:**
   - The derivative of \( u(x) = \cos(x) \) is:
     \[
     u'(x) = -\sin(x)
     \]
   - The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln(x) \) is:
     \[
     v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
     \]

3. **Apply the Product Rule:**
   \[
   f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives:
   \[
   f'(x) = (-\sin(x)) \cdot \ln(x) + \cos(x) \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
   \]

4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   \[
   f'(x) = -\sin(x)\ln(x) + \frac{\cos(x)}{x}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( f(x) = \cos(x) \cdot \ln(x) \) is:

\[
f'(x) = -\sin(x)\ln(x) + \frac{\cos(x)}{x}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use calculus rules. 

### Solution
Given:
\[
f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1}
\]

**Step 1:** Rewrite the function using a negative exponent.
\[
f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
\]

**Step 2:** Apply the chain rule to differentiate \( f(x) \).
- Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \), so \( f(x) = u^{-1} \).
- The derivative of \( u^{-1} \) with respect to \( u \) is:
\[
\frac{df}{du} = -u^{-2}
\]
- The derivative of \( u = x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
\frac{du}{dx} = 2x
\]

**Step 3:** Combine the derivatives using the chain rule.
\[
\frac{df}{dx} = \frac{df}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x
\]

**Step 4:** Substitute \( u = x^2 + 1 \) back into the equation.
\[
\frac{df}{dx} = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x = \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

### Final Answer
\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: \, \frac{-2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}
\]

1. Let u = sin x, so f(x) becomes ln(u).
2. The derivative of ln(u) with respect to u is 1/u.

The function f(x) = \(\frac{e^x}{1 + e^x}\) can be differentiated using the quotient rule. 

Let \( u = e^x \) and \( v = 1 + e^x \). Then:

- The derivative of \( u \) is \( u' = e^x \).
- The derivative of \( v \) is \( v' = e^x \).

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:
\[
e^x(1 + e^x) - e^{2x} = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x
\]

Thus, 
\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Answer:**  
The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \) is \( f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2} \).

{'Final Answer': 'e^x/(1+e^x)^2'}

The derivative of \(\tan^{-1} x\) with respect to \(x\) is found using the known result from calculus. 

**Solution:**
We know that if \( y = \tan^{-1} x \), then \( \tan y = x \). Differentiating both sides with respect to \(x\):

\[
\sec^2 y \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Solving for \(\frac{dy}{dx}\):

\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y}
\]

Since \(\cos^2 y = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}\) (derived from the right triangle where \(y = \tan^{-1} x\)), we have:

\[
\sec^2 y = 1 + x^2
\]

Thus,

\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**

So, the first derivative \( f'(x) \) will be \( 2x \cdot e^{x^2} \).

Next, I'll find the second derivative by differentiating \( f'(x) \). This involves applying the product rule to the terms \( 2x \) and \( e^{x^2} \). The derivatives are:

- The derivative of \( 2x \) is 2.
- The derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to x is \( 2x \cdot e^{x^2} \).

To find the second derivative of \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll follow these steps:

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative**

We start by finding the first derivative \( f'(x) \).

\[
f(x) = e^{x^2}
\]

Using the chain rule, where if \( u = x^2 \), then:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} e^{u} = e^{u} \cdot \frac{du}{dx}
\]

So,

\[
f'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative**

Next, we find the second derivative \( f''(x) \) by differentiating \( f'(x) \).

Given:

\[
f'(x) = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

We apply the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( h(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

Let:
- \( u(x) = 2x \) ⇒ \( u'(x) = 2 \)
- \( v(x) = e^{x^2} \) ⇒ \( v'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \)

Applying the product rule:

\[
f''(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 2 \cdot e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot (2x e^{x^2})
\]

Simplify:

\[
f''(x) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
\]

Factor out \( 2e^{x^2} \):

\[
f''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)
\]

**Final Answer**

The second derivative of \( f(x) = e^{x^2} \) is:

\[
\boxed{2e^{x^2}\left(1 + 2x^2\right)}
\]

Which is equal to tan x * sec x, as I thought earlier because:

tan x = sin x / cos x,
sec x = 1 / cos x,
so multiplying them gives (sin x / cos x) * (1 / cos x) = sin x / cos^2 x.

So that checks out. So the derivative of sec x is indeed sec x tan x, which I can write as d/dx (sec x) = sec x tan x.

Alternatively, thinking about the derivative from first principles might be too complicated, but maybe expanding in terms of sine and cosine:

d/dx (1 / cos x) = d/dx (sec x).

Using the chain rule: if f(g(x)) = 1/g(x), then f'(g(x)) * g'(x) is -1/(cos^2 x)*(-sin x) = sin x / cos^2 x, which again simplifies to sec x tan x.

Alright, so I feel confident that the derivative of sec x is sec x times tan x. So putting it all together, when someone asks for d/dx (sec x), the answer is sec x multiplied by tan x.

The derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using the known differentiation rule for reciprocal trigonometric functions.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express \(\sec x\) in terms of basic trigonometric functions:**
   \[
   \sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}
   \]

2. **Apply the differentiation rule for \(1/u(x)\):**
   The derivative of \(1/u(x)\) with respect to \(x\) is given by:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{u(x)}\right) = -\frac{u'(x)}{[u(x)]^2}
   \]
   
3. **Let \(u(x) = \cos x\) and find its derivative:**
   \[
   u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(\cos x) = -\sin x
   \]

4. **Substitute \(u(x)\) and \(u'(x)\) into the differentiation formula:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{\cos x}\right) = -\frac{-\sin x}{(\cos x)^2}
   \]
   
5. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   \frac{\sin x}{(\cos x)^2} = \sec x \cdot \tan x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\) is:
\[
\boxed{\sec x \cdot \tan x}
\]
